I used to tell patients that during long procedures, actually, because people always think the drilling is lasting forever.\n\nBut for me personally, I can’t think of a clear episode where fear suddenly built up and peaked in just a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, sort of the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair—explain the facts, remind myself what’s likely and what isn’t. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally pushed me to come in is that my concentration has gotten kind of unreliable. I’m a dentist, so I’ll be at the clinic reviewing a chart, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three times and still don’t know what it says. Or I’ll be at home trying to lay out a watercolor wash — I’ve done that for years, nothing fancy, just something I enjoy — and my mind is off somewhere else, running through everything that could go wrong. It’s frustrating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say. It kind of hops from one thing to another. I’ll start thinking about whether my old patients are being taken care of properly, even though I know they’re in capable hands, and then somehow I’m worrying about whether the practice handoff was financially sound, or whether I’m going to become sort of… useless once I’m fully retired.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, it’s probably bigger in my head than it needs to be. But it keeps circling anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, no. I mean, I get worked up about things and it can feel very uncomfortable, but I don’t think I’ve had it suddenly shoot up to a peak within a few minutes like you’re describing.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding—because there have been times at the clinic where I feel tense and distracted, and then I start thinking about a patient, and that leads to three other things… but it’s more of a building, circling kind of thing than a sudden episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not entirely sure. I mean, I don’t pace around the house dramatically or anything like that. My wife used to say I’d tap my fingers when I was thinking, but she said that about everyone, honestly.\n\nI do feel keyed up sometimes, I suppose, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “on edge.” It’s more like I have a hard time settling into things. Although, now that I say that, I’m thinking of how I used to sit so still doing crown work for hours—funny how that was never a problem at work when there was a patient in the chair. So… I guess I’m not sure how to answer that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding.” It’s more like I put things off because I can’t quite settle on what the right move is.\n\nWith the retirement handoff, for example, I’ve slowed parts of it down and postponed decisions because I keep second-guessing myself—whether I explained something properly, whether the numbers make sense, whether I’m leaving things in good shape. And then I lose time at the clinic going over things that probably don’t need that much rechecking.\n\nEven things I used to just enjoy, like painting or gardening, don’t feel as manageable lately. I’ll stand there thinking I should start, and then somehow it feels like too much, or I get pulled back into worrying about the practice. My tomatoes used to be almost therapeutic, you know—well, except the year the squirrels got them all, that was a battle. But yes, I guess I have been postponing or avoiding things more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not out of nowhere like that.\n\nI can get very uncomfortable, but usually there’s a thread I can follow, you know? Like something at the practice, or a decision I haven’t made, and then my mind starts pulling at it. It’s not like I’m sitting there perfectly fine and suddenly I’m hit by fear. At least, I can’t think of a time like that.\n\nThough sometimes I wonder if I’m just so used to being wound up that I don’t notice the starting point anymore. But “sudden” and “out of nowhere” doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tense in your muscles?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension… I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’m 64, so things are stiff here and there anyway, especially if I’ve been sitting too long. Dentistry isn’t exactly kind to the neck and shoulders—years of leaning over patients, you know, that probably adds up.\n\nBut whether I “often” feel tense in my muscles because of worry… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it that way. Sometimes I notice my shoulders are up around my ears, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just posture. I had an assistant once who used to remind me to lower them—funny, she’d say it like I was bracing for impact during a filling.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I can’t say clearly. It’s not something I’ve really tracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to peak within a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. “Peak within a few minutes” sounds very… specific, like there’s a clear beginning and high point, and I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nWhen I get worked up, it tends to feel more like I’ve been chewing on something for a while and then realize I’m uncomfortable. Not like a switch flips. Although time can get funny when you’re upset—five minutes can feel like half an hour, or the other way around. I used to tell patients that during long procedures, actually, because people always think the drilling is lasting forever.\n\nBut for me personally, I can’t think of a clear episode where fear suddenly built up and peaked in just a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, sort of the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair—explain the facts, remind myself what’s likely and what isn’t. I used to be pretty good at that with patients, actually. You learn to keep your voice calm even when the suction’s clogging or someone’s running late—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, with myself it doesn’t always work. One worry turns into the next one, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, looking back over notes, or asking the same question I already asked because I can’t quite let it sit. It’s like I’m trying to get certainty, but I don’t really get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a panic attack. I mean, I’ve heard patients use that phrase—people would come into the office already white-knuckling the armrests before I’d even picked up an instrument, and they’d say, “I’m having a panic attack,” but I never really knew exactly where the line was.\n\nFor me, I’ve had times where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, sure, but whether that counts as a panic attack… I’m not sure. It doesn’t feel like something I could point to and say, “Yes, that was definitely it.” I suppose I’d need you to tell me what you mean by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, because some of the things are real concerns. I mean, the practice handoff does matter financially, and I do care whether my old patients are being looked after properly. You spend years knowing Mrs. Klein needs extra time because she gets nervous, or that Mr. Alvarez never tells you when something hurts until it’s really bad—sorry, that’s probably beside the point.\n\nBut yes, the worry feels bigger than the actual problems. My mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and it doesn’t stop at “I hope they’re okay.” It becomes, “What if I should have done more?” or “What if I’m useless once I’m fully retired?” Even as I’m thinking it, part of me knows it’s out of proportion. I just can’t always make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack that you would describe as such?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes to that. I’ve been very anxious—wound up, like I can’t quite settle—but “panic attack” feels like a specific thing, and I don’t know that I’ve had one I’d confidently call that.\n\nI mean, in dentistry, people use the term in all sorts of ways. Some would say it when they were just extremely scared before a procedure, and others seemed… well, really overtaken by it. So when you ask me, I hesitate. I don’t want to label it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find it hard to stop worrying once you start?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to talk myself down, you know, the way I used to with a nervous patient in the chair—calm voice, one step at a time, “Let’s just look at what we know.” That used to work fairly well for other people.\n\nBut with me, one concern sort of opens the door to the next one. I’ll tell myself I’m only going to check one email, and then I’m looking through old notes, or wondering if I followed up properly, or asking the same question again even though someone’s already answered me. It’s hard to just… let it sit and move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that seemed to come on very quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’ve had times where I suddenly felt very uncomfortable, yes, but I don’t know if I’d call it overwhelming in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s funny, because with patients I could usually tell when fear came over them quickly—the hands gripping the armrests, that look in the eyes. I remember one man who insisted he was fine right up until I picked up the drill, and then—well, anyway, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nFor me, it’s more like I realize I’m already wound up, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice a clear “it came out of nowhere” moment. Or at least I haven’t paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has. Not in some dramatic way, maybe, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nI’ve had to slow down the retirement handoff at the practice—postpone pieces of it—because I keep second-guessing decisions. I’ll think I’ve settled something, and then I lose time at the clinic going back over it again. And then, you know, that affects the person taking over, the staff, all of that.\n\nEven at home, things I used to find steadying—painting, gardening—don’t feel as manageable as they used to. I’ll go out to the garden and somehow spend more time fussing over whether I’m doing it right than actually enjoying it. Which sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud, but yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it does. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll be any use once I’m fully retired. And I know, rationally, some of it is probably out of proportion. I mean, I had patients for years who worried a crown would fall out the second they left the office, and I’d reassure them, “No, no, it’s sound, it’s been checked”—and now I’m doing the same sort of thing in my own head, just with different subjects.\n\nIt’s distressing because I can see I’m not thinking clearly about it, but I can’t quite set it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. Maybe not every single minute, but it’s there for a lot of the day, circling in the background.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary and then I’m back to wondering whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I’ll just become useless once I’m fully retired. And I know some of it is out of proportion—if a patient had talked to me that way, I’d probably have tried to gently reassure them—but knowing that doesn’t seem to stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who paces around or can’t sit still, exactly.

I mean, I’ve always been fairly used to being on my feet—dentistry isn’t exactly a sitting-and-staring sort of profession, even though people imagine you just sit on that little stool all day. So… maybe a little sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed.

But “sudden” and “out of nowhere” doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tense in your muscles?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension… I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’m 64, so things are stiff here and there anyway, especially if I’ve been sitting too long. Dentistry isn’t exactly kind to the neck and shoulders—years of leaning over patients, you know, that probably adds up.\n\nBut whether I “often” feel tense in my muscles because of worry… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it that way. Sometimes I notice my shoulders are up around my ears, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just posture. I had an assistant once who used to remind me to lower them—funny, she’d say it like I was bracing for impact during a filling.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I can’t say clearly. It’s not something I’ve really tracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to peak within a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. “Peak within a few minutes” sounds very… specific, like there’s a clear beginning and high point, and I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nWhen I get worked up, it tends to feel more like I’ve been chewing on something for a while and then realize I’m uncomfortable. Not like a switch flips. Although time can get funny when you’re upset—five minutes can feel like half an hour, or the other way around. I used to tell patients that during long procedures, actually, because people always think the drilling is lasting forever.\n\nBut for me personally, I can’t think of a clear episode where fear suddenly built up and peaked in just a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, sort of the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair—explain the facts, remind myself what’s likely and what isn’t. I used to be pretty good at that with patients, actually. You learn to keep your voice calm even when the suction’s clogging or someone’s running late—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, with myself it doesn’t always work. One worry turns into the next one, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, looking back over notes, or asking the same question I already asked because I can’t quite let it sit. It’s like I’m trying to get certainty, but I don’t really get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a panic attack. I mean, I’ve heard patients use that phrase—people would come into the office already white-knuckling the armrests before I’d even picked up an instrument, and they’d say, “I’m having a panic attack,” but I never really knew exactly where the line was.\n\nFor me, I’ve had times where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, sure, but whether that counts as a panic attack… I’m not sure. It doesn’t feel like something I could point to and say, “Yes, that was definitely it.” I suppose I’d need you to tell me what you mean by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, because some of the things are real concerns. I mean, the practice handoff does matter financially, and I do care whether my old patients are being looked after properly. You spend years knowing Mrs. Klein needs extra time because she gets nervous, or that Mr. Alvarez never tells you when something hurts until it’s really bad—sorry, that’s probably beside the point.\n\nBut yes, the worry feels bigger than the actual problems. My mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and it doesn’t stop at “I hope they’re okay.” It becomes, “What if I should have done more?” or “What if I’m useless once I’m fully retired?” Even as I’m thinking it, part of me knows it’s out of proportion. I just can’t always make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack that you would describe as such?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes to that. I’ve been very anxious—wound up, like I can’t quite settle—but “panic attack” feels like a specific thing, and I don’t know that I’ve had one I’d confidently call that.\n\nI mean, in dentistry, people use the term in all sorts of ways. Some would say it when they were just extremely scared before a procedure, and others seemed… well, really overtaken by it. So when you ask me, I hesitate. I don’t want to label it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find it hard to stop worrying once you start?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to talk myself down, you know, the way I used to with a nervous patient in the chair—calm voice, one step at a time, “Let’s just look at what we know.” That used to work fairly well for other people.\n\nBut with me, one concern sort of opens the door to the next one. I’ll tell myself I’m only going to check one email, and then I’m looking through old notes, or wondering if I followed up properly, or asking the same question again even though someone’s already answered me. It’s hard to just… let it sit and move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that seemed to come on very quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’ve had times where I suddenly felt very uncomfortable, yes, but I don’t know if I’d call it overwhelming in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s funny, because with patients I could usually tell when fear came over them quickly—the hands gripping the armrests, that look in the eyes. I remember one man who insisted he was fine right up until I picked up the drill, and then—well, anyway, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nFor me, it’s more like I realize I’m already wound up, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice a clear “it came out of nowhere” moment. Or at least I haven’t paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has. Not in some dramatic way, maybe, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nI’ve had to slow down the retirement handoff at the practice—postpone pieces of it—because I keep second-guessing decisions. I’ll think I’ve settled something, and then I lose time at the clinic going back over it again. And then, you know, that affects the person taking over, the staff, all of that.\n\nEven at home, things I used to find steadying—painting, gardening—don’t feel as manageable as they used to. I’ll go out to the garden and somehow spend more time fussing over whether I’m doing it right than actually enjoying it. Which sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud, but yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it does. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll be any use once I’m fully retired. And I know, rationally, some of it is probably out of proportion. I mean, I had patients for years who worried a crown would fall out the second they left the office, and I’d reassure them, “No, no, it’s sound, it’s been checked”—and now I’m doing the same sort of thing in my own head, just with different subjects.\n\nIt’s distressing because I can see I’m not thinking clearly about it, but I can’t quite set it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. Maybe not every single minute, but it’s there for a lot of the day, circling in the background.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary and then I’m back to wondering whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I’ll just become useless once I’m fully retired. And I know some of it is out of proportion—if a patient had talked to me that way, I’d probably have tried to gently reassure them—but knowing that doesn’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you can't sit still or have an urge to move around when you're feeling worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who paces around or can’t sit still, exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve always been fairly used to being on my feet—dentistry isn’t exactly a sitting-and-staring sort of profession, even though people imagine you just sit on that little stool all day. So I’m not sure I’d notice it as unusual. When I’m worried, I might get up and fuss with something, or straighten a few things, but I don’t know if that’s the same as what you mean.\n\nSo… maybe a little sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Generalized Anxiety Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it does. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll be any use once I’m fully retired. And I know, rationally, some of it is probably out of proportion. I mean, I had patients for years who worried a crown would fall out the second they left the office, and I’d reassure them, “No, no, it’s sound, it’s been checked”—and now I’m doing the same sort of thing in my own head, just with different subjects.\n\nIt’s distressing because I can see I’m not thinking clearly about it, but I can’t quite set it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. Maybe not every single minute, but it’s there for a lot of the day, circling in the background.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary and then I’m back to wondering whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I’ll just become useless once I’m fully retired. And I know some of it is out of proportion—if a patient had talked to me that way, I’d probably have tried to gently reassure them—but knowing that doesn’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you can't sit still or have an urge to move around when you're feeling worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who paces around or can’t sit still, exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve always been fairly used to being on my feet—dentistry isn’t exactly a sitting-and-staring sort of profession, even though people imagine you just sit on that little stool all day. So I’m not sure I’d notice it as unusual. When I’m worried, I might get up and fuss with something, or straighten a few things, but I don’t know if that’s the same as what you mean.\n\nSo… maybe a little sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel like you're fidgety when you're worried?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 64 / dentist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Fidgety… hm. I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly.

I suppose I might fiddle with a pen or tap my fingers a bit, but then again, I did that for years at the practice—always checking instruments, adjusting trays, you know, tiny movements all day long. Dentists develop these little hand habits, I think. My wife used to say I couldn’t watch television without picking at something on the side table.

But whether it’s specifically when I’m worried… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way. Maybe a little, but it’s not something I’d have come in complaining about.

---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally pushed me to come in is that my concentration has gotten kind of unreliable. I’m a dentist, so I’ll be at the clinic reviewing a chart, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three times and still don’t know what it says. Or I’ll be at home trying to lay out a watercolor wash — I’ve done that for years, nothing fancy, just something I enjoy — and my mind is off somewhere else, running through everything that could go wrong. It’s frustrating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say. It kind of hops from one thing to another. I’ll start thinking about whether my old patients are being taken care of properly, even though I know they’re in capable hands, and then somehow I’m worrying about whether the practice handoff was financially sound, or whether I’m going to become sort of… useless once I’m fully retired.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, it’s probably bigger in my head than it needs to be. But it keeps circling anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, no. I mean, I get worked up about things and it can feel very uncomfortable, but I don’t think I’ve had it suddenly shoot up to a peak within a few minutes like you’re describing.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding—because there have been times at the clinic where I feel tense and distracted, and then I start thinking about a patient, and that leads to three other things… but it’s more of a building, circling kind of thing than a sudden episode.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not entirely sure. I mean, I don’t pace around the house dramatically or anything like that. My wife used to say I’d tap my fingers when I was thinking, but she said that about everyone, honestly.\n\nI do feel keyed up sometimes, I suppose, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “on edge.” It’s more like I have a hard time settling into things. Although, now that I say that, I’m thinking of how I used to sit so still doing crown work for hours—funny how that was never a problem at work when there was a patient in the chair. So… I guess I’m not sure how to answer that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding.” It’s more like I put things off because I can’t quite settle on what the right move is.\n\nWith the retirement handoff, for example, I’ve slowed parts of it down and postponed decisions because I keep second-guessing myself—whether I explained something properly, whether the numbers make sense, whether I’m leaving things in good shape. And then I lose time at the clinic going over things that probably don’t need that much rechecking.\n\nEven things I used to just enjoy, like painting or gardening, don’t feel as manageable lately. I’ll stand there thinking I should start, and then somehow it feels like too much, or I get pulled back into worrying about the practice. My tomatoes used to be almost therapeutic, you know—well, except the year the squirrels got them all, that was a battle. But yes, I guess I have been postponing or avoiding things more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not out of nowhere like that.\n\nI can get very uncomfortable, but usually there’s a thread I can follow, you know? Like something at the practice, or a decision I haven’t made, and then my mind starts pulling at it. It’s not like I’m sitting there perfectly fine and suddenly I’m hit by fear. At least, I can’t think of a time like that.\n\nThough sometimes I wonder if I’m just so used to being wound up that I don’t notice the starting point anymore. But “sudden” and “out of nowhere” doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tense in your muscles?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension… I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’m 64, so things are stiff here and there anyway, especially if I’ve been sitting too long. Dentistry isn’t exactly kind to the neck and shoulders—years of leaning over patients, you know, that probably adds up.\n\nBut whether I “often” feel tense in my muscles because of worry… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it that way. Sometimes I notice my shoulders are up around my ears, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just posture. I had an assistant once who used to remind me to lower them—funny, she’d say it like I was bracing for impact during a filling.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I can’t say clearly. It’s not something I’ve really tracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to peak within a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. “Peak within a few minutes” sounds very… specific, like there’s a clear beginning and high point, and I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nWhen I get worked up, it tends to feel more like I’ve been chewing on something for a while and then realize I’m uncomfortable. Not like a switch flips. Although time can get funny when you’re upset—five minutes can feel like half an hour, or the other way around. I used to tell patients that during long procedures, actually, because people always think the drilling is lasting forever.\n\nBut for me personally, I can’t think of a clear episode where fear suddenly built up and peaked in just a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, sort of the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair—explain the facts, remind myself what’s likely and what isn’t. I used to be pretty good at that with patients, actually. You learn to keep your voice calm even when the suction’s clogging or someone’s running late—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, with myself it doesn’t always work. One worry turns into the next one, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, looking back over notes, or asking the same question I already asked because I can’t quite let it sit. It’s like I’m trying to get certainty, but I don’t really get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a panic attack. I mean, I’ve heard patients use that phrase—people would come into the office already white-knuckling the armrests before I’d even picked up an instrument, and they’d say, “I’m having a panic attack,” but I never really knew exactly where the line was.\n\nFor me, I’ve had times where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, sure, but whether that counts as a panic attack… I’m not sure. It doesn’t feel like something I could point to and say, “Yes, that was definitely it.” I suppose I’d need you to tell me what you mean by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, because some of the things are real concerns. I mean, the practice handoff does matter financially, and I do care whether my old patients are being looked after properly. You spend years knowing Mrs. Klein needs extra time because she gets nervous, or that Mr. Alvarez never tells you when something hurts until it’s really bad—sorry, that’s probably beside the point.\n\nBut yes, the worry feels bigger than the actual problems. My mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and it doesn’t stop at “I hope they’re okay.” It becomes, “What if I should have done more?” or “What if I’m useless once I’m fully retired?” Even as I’m thinking it, part of me knows it’s out of proportion. I just can’t always make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack that you would describe as such?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes to that. I’ve been very anxious—wound up, like I can’t quite settle—but “panic attack” feels like a specific thing, and I don’t know that I’ve had one I’d confidently call that.\n\nI mean, in dentistry, people use the term in all sorts of ways. Some would say it when they were just extremely scared before a procedure, and others seemed… well, really overtaken by it. So when you ask me, I hesitate. I don’t want to label it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find it hard to stop worrying once you start?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to talk myself down, you know, the way I used to with a nervous patient in the chair—calm voice, one step at a time, “Let’s just look at what we know.” That used to work fairly well for other people.\n\nBut with me, one concern sort of opens the door to the next one. I’ll tell myself I’m only going to check one email, and then I’m looking through old notes, or wondering if I followed up properly, or asking the same question again even though someone’s already answered me. It’s hard to just… let it sit and move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that seemed to come on very quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’ve had times where I suddenly felt very uncomfortable, yes, but I don’t know if I’d call it overwhelming in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s funny, because with patients I could usually tell when fear came over them quickly—the hands gripping the armrests, that look in the eyes. I remember one man who insisted he was fine right up until I picked up the drill, and then—well, anyway, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nFor me, it’s more like I realize I’m already wound up, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice a clear “it came out of nowhere” moment. Or at least I haven’t paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has. Not in some dramatic way, maybe, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nI’ve had to slow down the retirement handoff at the practice—postpone pieces of it—because I keep second-guessing decisions. I’ll think I’ve settled something, and then I lose time at the clinic going back over it again. And then, you know, that affects the person taking over, the staff, all of that.\n\nEven at home, things I used to find steadying—painting, gardening—don’t feel as manageable as they used to. I’ll go out to the garden and somehow spend more time fussing over whether I’m doing it right than actually enjoying it. Which sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud, but yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it does. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll be any use once I’m fully retired. And I know, rationally, some of it is probably out of proportion. I mean, I had patients for years who worried a crown would fall out the second they left the office, and I’d reassure them, “No, no, it’s sound, it’s been checked”—and now I’m doing the same sort of thing in my own head, just with different subjects.\n\nIt’s distressing because I can see I’m not thinking clearly about it, but I can’t quite set it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. Maybe not every single minute, but it’s there for a lot of the day, circling in the background.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary and then I’m back to wondering whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I’ll just become useless once I’m fully retired. And I know some of it is out of proportion—if a patient had talked to me that way, I’d probably have tried to gently reassure them—but knowing that doesn’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you can't sit still or have an urge to move around when you're feeling worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who paces around or can’t sit still, exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve always been fairly used to being on my feet—dentistry isn’t exactly a sitting-and-staring sort of profession, even though people imagine you just sit on that little stool all day. So I’m not sure I’d notice it as unusual. When I’m worried, I might get up and fuss with something, or straighten a few things, but I don’t know if that’s the same as what you mean.\n\nSo… maybe a little sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgety when you're worried?\n\nPatient: Fidgety… hm. I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly.\n\nI suppose I might fiddle with a pen or tap my fingers a bit, but then again, I did that for years at the practice—always checking instruments, adjusting trays, you know, tiny movements all day long. Dentists develop these little hand habits, I think. My wife used to say I couldn’t watch television without picking at something on the side table.\n\nBut whether it’s specifically when I’m worried… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way. Maybe a little, but it’s not something I’d have come in complaining about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I try to talk myself down, you know, the way I used to with a nervous patient in the chair—calm voice, one step at a time, “Let’s just look at what we know.” That used to work fairly well for other people.\n\nBut with me, one concern sort of opens the door to the next one. I’ll tell myself I’m only going to check one email, and then I’m looking through old notes, or wondering if I followed up properly, or asking the same question again even though someone’s already answered me. It’s hard to just… let it sit and move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that seemed to come on very quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’ve had times where I suddenly felt very uncomfortable, yes, but I don’t know if I’d call it overwhelming in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s funny, because with patients I could usually tell when fear came over them quickly—the hands gripping the armrests, that look in the eyes. I remember one man who insisted he was fine right up until I picked up the drill, and then—well, anyway, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nFor me, it’s more like I realize I’m already wound up, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice a clear “it came out of nowhere” moment. Or at least I haven’t paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has. Not in some dramatic way, maybe, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nI’ve had to slow down the retirement handoff at the practice—postpone pieces of it—because I keep second-guessing decisions. I’ll think I’ve settled something, and then I lose time at the clinic going back over it again. And then, you know, that affects the person taking over, the staff, all of that.\n\nEven at home, things I used to find steadying—painting, gardening—don’t feel as manageable as they used to. I’ll go out to the garden and somehow spend more time fussing over whether I’m doing it right than actually enjoying it. Which sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud, but yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it does. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll be any use once I’m fully retired. And I know, rationally, some of it is probably out of proportion. I mean, I had patients for years who worried a crown would fall out the second they left the office, and I’d reassure them, “No, no, it’s sound, it’s been checked”—and now I’m doing the same sort of thing in my own head, just with different subjects.\n\nIt’s distressing because I can see I’m not thinking clearly about it, but I can’t quite set it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. Maybe not every single minute, but it’s there for a lot of the day, circling in the background.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary and then I’m back to wondering whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I’ll just become useless once I’m fully retired. And I know some of it is out of proportion—if a patient had talked to me that way, I’d probably have tried to gently reassure them—but knowing that doesn’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you can't sit still or have an urge to move around when you're feeling worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who paces around or can’t sit still, exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve always been fairly used to being on my feet—dentistry isn’t exactly a sitting-and-staring sort of profession, even though people imagine you just sit on that little stool all day. So I’m not sure I’d notice it as unusual. When I’m worried, I might get up and fuss with something, or straighten a few things, but I don’t know if that’s the same as what you mean.\n\nSo… maybe a little sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgety when you're worried?\n\nPatient: Fidgety… hm. I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly.\n\nI suppose I might fiddle with a pen or tap my fingers a bit, but then again, I did that for years at the practice—always checking instruments, adjusting trays, you know, tiny movements all day long. Dentists develop these little hand habits, I think. My wife used to say I couldn’t watch television without picking at something on the side table.\n\nBut whether it’s specifically when I’m worried… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way. Maybe a little, but it’s not something I’d have come in complaining about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally pushed me to come in is that my concentration has gotten kind of unreliable. I’m a dentist, so I’ll be at the clinic reviewing a chart, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three times and still don’t know what it says. Or I’ll be at home trying to lay out a watercolor wash — I’ve done that for years, nothing fancy, just something I enjoy — and my mind is off somewhere else, running through everything that could go wrong. It’s frustrating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say. It kind of hops from one thing to another. I’ll start thinking about whether my old patients are being taken care of properly, even though I know they’re in capable hands, and then somehow I’m worrying about whether the practice handoff was financially sound, or whether I’m going to become sort of… useless once I’m fully retired.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, it’s probably bigger in my head than it needs to be. So… I guess I’m not sure how to answer that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding.” It’s more like I put things off because I can’t quite settle on what the right move is.\n\nWith the retirement handoff, for example, I’ve slowed parts of it down and postponed decisions because I keep second-guessing myself—whether I explained something properly, whether the numbers make sense, whether I’m leaving things in good shape. And then I lose time at the clinic going over things that probably don’t need that much rechecking.\n\nEven things I used to just enjoy, like painting or gardening, don’t feel as manageable lately. I’ll stand there thinking I should start, and then somehow it feels like too much, or I get pulled back into worrying about the practice. My tomatoes used to be almost therapeutic, you know—well, except the year the squirrels got them all, that was a battle. But yes, I guess I have been postponing or avoiding things more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not out of nowhere like that.\n\nI can get very uncomfortable, but usually there’s a thread I can follow, you know? Like something at the practice, or a decision I haven’t made, and then my mind starts pulling at it. It’s not like I’m sitting there perfectly fine and suddenly I’m hit by fear. At least, I can’t think of a time like that.\n\nThough sometimes I wonder if I’m just so used to being wound up that I don’t notice the starting point anymore. But “sudden” and “out of nowhere” doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tense in your muscles?\n\nPatient: Muscle tension… I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’m 64, so things are stiff here and there anyway, especially if I’ve been sitting too long. Sometimes I notice my shoulders are up around my ears, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just posture. I had an assistant once who used to remind me to lower them—funny, she’d say it like I was bracing for impact during a filling.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I can’t say clearly. It’s not something I’ve really tracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to peak within a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. “Peak within a few minutes” sounds very… specific, like there’s a clear beginning and high point, and I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nWhen I get worked up, it tends to feel more like I’ve been chewing on something for a while and then realize I’m uncomfortable. Not like a switch flips. Although time can get funny when you’re upset—five minutes can feel like half an hour, or the other way around. I used to tell patients that during long procedures, actually, because people always think the drilling is lasting forever.\n\nBut for me personally, I can’t think of a clear episode where fear suddenly built up and peaked in just a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control your worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, more than I like to admit.\n\nI’ll try to talk myself down, sort of the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair—explain the facts, remind myself what’s likely and what isn’t. I used to be pretty good at that with patients, actually. You learn to keep your voice calm even when the suction’s clogging or someone’s running late—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, with myself it doesn’t always work. One worry turns into the next one, and before I know it I’m checking emails again, looking back over notes, or asking the same question I already asked because I can’t quite let it sit. It’s like I’m trying to get certainty, but I don’t really get there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a panic attack. I mean, I’ve heard patients use that phrase—people would come into the office already white-knuckling the armrests before I’d even picked up an instrument, and they’d say, “I’m having a panic attack,” but I never really knew exactly where the line was.\n\nFor me, I’ve had times where I feel very wound up or overwhelmed, sure, but whether that counts as a panic attack… I’m not sure. It doesn’t feel like something I could point to and say, “Yes, that was definitely it.” I suppose I’d need you to tell me what you mean by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is disproportionate to the actual problems you're facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t like admitting that, because some of the things are real concerns. I mean, the practice handoff does matter financially, and I do care whether my old patients are being looked after properly. You spend years knowing Mrs. Klein needs extra time because she gets nervous, or that Mr. Alvarez never tells you when something hurts until it’s really bad—sorry, that’s probably beside the point.\n\nBut yes, the worry feels bigger than the actual problems. My mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and it doesn’t stop at “I hope they’re okay.” It becomes, “What if I should have done more?” or “What if I’m useless once I’m fully retired?” Even as I’m thinking it, part of me knows it’s out of proportion. I just can’t always make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack that you would describe as such?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes to that. I’ve been very anxious—wound up, like I can’t quite settle—but “panic attack” feels like a specific thing, and I don’t know that I’ve had one I’d confidently call that.\n\nI mean, in dentistry, people use the term in all sorts of ways. Some would say it when they were just extremely scared before a procedure, and others seemed… well, really overtaken by it. So when you ask me, I hesitate. I don’t want to label it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find it hard to stop worrying once you start?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I try to talk myself down, you know, the way I used to with a nervous patient in the chair—calm voice, one step at a time, “Let’s just look at what we know.” That used to work fairly well for other people.\n\nBut with me, one concern sort of opens the door to the next one. I’ll tell myself I’m only going to check one email, and then I’m looking through old notes, or wondering if I followed up properly, or asking the same question again even though someone’s already answered me. It’s hard to just… let it sit and move on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that seemed to come on very quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’ve had times where I suddenly felt very uncomfortable, yes, but I don’t know if I’d call it overwhelming in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s funny, because with patients I could usually tell when fear came over them quickly—the hands gripping the armrests, that look in the eyes. I remember one man who insisted he was fine right up until I picked up the drill, and then—well, anyway, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nFor me, it’s more like I realize I’m already wound up, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice a clear “it came out of nowhere” moment. Or at least I haven’t paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has. Not in some dramatic way, maybe, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nI’ve had to slow down the retirement handoff at the practice—postpone pieces of it—because I keep second-guessing decisions. I’ll think I’ve settled something, and then I lose time at the clinic going back over it again. And then, you know, that affects the person taking over, the staff, all of that.\n\nEven at home, things I used to find steadying—painting, gardening—don’t feel as manageable as they used to. I’ll go out to the garden and somehow spend more time fussing over whether I’m doing it right than actually enjoying it. Which sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud, but yes, it’s interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it does. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll be any use once I’m fully retired. And I know, rationally, some of it is probably out of proportion. I mean, I had patients for years who worried a crown would fall out the second they left the office, and I’d reassure them, “No, no, it’s sound, it’s been checked”—and now I’m doing the same sort of thing in my own head, just with different subjects.\n\nIt’s distressing because I can see I’m not thinking clearly about it, but I can’t quite set it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. Maybe not every single minute, but it’s there for a lot of the day, circling in the background.\n\nI’ll be doing something ordinary and then I’m back to wondering whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or whether the practice handoff is financially sound, or whether I’ll just become useless once I’m fully retired. And I know some of it is out of proportion—if a patient had talked to me that way, I’d probably have tried to gently reassure them—but knowing that doesn’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you can't sit still or have an urge to move around when you're feeling worried?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who paces around or can’t sit still, exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve always been fairly used to being on my feet—dentistry isn’t exactly a sitting-and-staring sort of profession, even though people imagine you just sit on that little stool all day. So I’m not sure I’d notice it as unusual. When I’m worried, I might get up and fuss with something, or straighten a few things, but I don’t know if that’s the same as what you mean.\n\nSo… maybe a little sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgety when you're worried?\n\nPatient: Fidgety… hm. I’m not sure I’d call it that, exactly.\n\nI suppose I might fiddle with a pen or tap my fingers a bit, but then again, I did that for years at the practice—always checking instruments, adjusting trays, you know, tiny movements all day long. Dentists develop these little hand habits, I think. My wife used to say I couldn’t watch television without picking at something on the side table.\n\nBut whether it’s specifically when I’m worried… I don’t know. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way. Maybe a little, but it’s not something I’d have come in complaining about.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry significantly impacted your ability to function at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has, though I keep wanting to say “not too badly,” because I’m still functioning, you know. But at the clinic I’ve definitely slowed things down. The retirement handoff should have been more straightforward, and I keep second-guessing decisions—financial bits, patient continuity, whether I’ve explained things properly—and then I lose half a morning going over something that probably didn’t need going over again.\n\nAnd at home too, a bit. I used to paint to switch off, and I’ve always liked gardening—my father grew roses, actually, and he was very particular about pruning, far more than I ever was—but lately even those things feel harder to manage. Not impossible, just… they don’t feel as easy or settling as they used to. So yes, it has got in the way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
